10 Nov 2026Amsterdam UMC

Machine learning reveals incomplete concordance between DNASE1L3 genotype and cfDNA phenotype

Classifiers and UMAP analyzed 129,676 prenatal-test cfDNA samples, with genotype checks in 169 selected samples. The authors report fragmentomic profiles resembling DNASE1L3 R206C homozygotes in some non-homozygotes, while some confirmed homozygotes initially lacked the profile and acquired it in later pregnancies. This extends an already-known genotype association with phenotype discordance and within-person variation; it does not establish the mechanism or clinical utility.

Technology: Supervised classifiers; UMAP

  • AI role: Classifiers and low-dimensional embeddings identified fragmentomic subgroups and discordant cases.

9 Nov 2026Harvard Medical School; Harvard College; New York Genome Center; Rush Alzheimer’s Disease Center, Rush University

TriSCOPE links cell-type-specific Alzheimer’s expression programs to inherited risk loci

TriSCOPE combined cell-level predictive modeling, donor-aware differential expression and pseudotime analysis across six single-nucleus RNA-sequencing datasets (about 4.2 million nuclei from 627 donors). It identified cell-type-specific predictively expressed genes that were often missed by univariate differential-expression analysis; conditional GWAS–eQTL colocalization supported 27 gene–cell-type pairs at Alzheimer’s risk loci, including pairs without significant differential expression. Selected gene signals and analytical branches were compared across independent cohorts.

Technology: TriSCOPE, using LightGBM-based multivariate predictive modeling integrated with differential expression and pseudotime analysis

Relevance 53 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Selected gene signals and analytical branches were compared across independent cohorts.
  • Still unknown: This is a computational reanalysis of previously published, post-mortem cross-sectional datasets; it did not collect new tissue or perform perturbation experiments. The authors state the framework is for feature discovery rather than clinical prognosis, cannot establish temporal ordering, and requires functional perturbation studies to distinguish pathogenic drivers from protective or reactive signals. The reported cell-level AUCs are modest and should not be presented as patient-level diagnostic performance.
  • AI role: The supervised machine-learning branch prioritized gene-expression features that predicted clinical Alzheimer’s diagnosis within cell types, conditional on demographics and APOE genotype. The study integrated those model-ranked features with donor-aware differential expression, pseudotime and GWAS–eQTL colocalization to nominate genetically supported cell-type programs.

Evidence sources: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41467-026-77651-5.pdf

5 Nov 2026Texas A&M AgriLife Research; and Washington University School of Medicine in St. Louis

AlphaFold-Multimer predicts 2,895 interactions among human mitochondrial proteins

Texas A&M AgriLife Research screened 630,003 protein pairs from about 1,100 human mitochondrial proteins with AlphaFold-Multimer and identified 2,895 likely interactions, more than half not previously reported, giving 85 uncharacterized proteins their first probable partners. The paper appeared in Nature Communications on 25 August 2026 and the institutional release on 5 November. Lab experiments in yeast and human cells confirmed several of the partnerships; most predictions remain untested, and the disease links are potential targets, not established ones. Predictions are shared through the MitoMatch web tool.

Technology: AlphaFold-Multimer

  • Still unknown: Only several predictions were confirmed, most remain unverified, the interactions are described as likely rather than established, and disease links are potential targets.
  • AI role: AlphaFold-Multimer predicted pairwise interactions among mitochondrial proteins; the predictions were released as the MitoMatch web resource.

4 Nov 2026Anthropic

First 3D structure of a prokaryotic vault particle, with Claude

Claude searched protein databases, designed DNA constructs, reduced purification from about three days to about 90 minutes, and processed cryo-EM data into an initial model in 34 hours. The result is a particle made of 96 copies of a bacteriophage protein, with about 25% more internal volume than the mammalian version. Its biological function is unknown; this is a preprint, and Claude made errors that humans corrected.

Technology: Claude

Family Claude: profile · models page

  • Publication status: Preprint
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: The 3D structure was determined by cryo-EM; cryo-EM confirmed the vault shape for four of seven particle-forming proteins, and fifteen altered protein versions were tested, with a reviewer checking the structure.
  • Still unknown: The biological function is unknown, vaults were made only in E. coli, and an unidentified pocket molecule resembling coenzyme A is unconfirmed.
  • AI role: Claude searched protein databases, designed DNA constructs, optimized purification, and ran RELION cryo-EM processing to an initial model in 34 hours; it also made errors that humans corrected.

Evidence sources: https://www.anthropic.com/research/investigating-vault-particles-with-claude

29 Oct 2026Arc Institute; Stanford University

Evo 2 uses in-context learning to design de novo regulatory sequences with only tens of examples

Arc Institute reports that Evo 2 designs functional regulatory sequences from 4 to 64 in-context examples without extra training, with 30-50% yield. Of nine AI-designed enhancers delivered by AAV to mice, seven drove detectable expression and two labeled layer 5 extratelencephalic neurons with 73% and 95% specificity. Results are bioRxiv preprints; in vivo work covers only layer 5 enhancers, and other designs (proteins, CRISPR loci, antibodies, phage genomes) are computational only.

Technology: Evo 2 (genomic language model); in-context learning; reinforcement learning post-training

  • AI role: Evo 2 generated regulatory sequences from 4 to 64 in-context examples; reinforcement learning with predictive reward models sharpened cell-type specificity.

21 Oct 2026OpenAI; PerturbAI and Scripps Research

GPT-Rosalind agents connect genetic perturbations and neuronal signals

Analysis of a CRISPR atlas of mouse brain cells identified differences between Tsc1 and Tsc2 and a possible relationship between DCTN1 and cholesterol regulation. Experts assessed the interpretations, but the proposed mechanisms remain hypotheses to be tested in new experiments.

Technology: GPT-Rosalind and distributed agents

  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Thousands of GPT-Rosalind agents reanalyzed an existing whole-brain CRISPR atlas; they reproduced findings from human analyses, and their novelty and value assessments aligned with blinded human experts.
  • Still unknown: The proposed biological mechanisms still need experiments to test them.
  • AI role: Each agent investigated one gene disruption, searched the literature and proposed explanations; reports were combined into a reasoning atlas.

21 Oct 2026Westlake University; and collaborating institutions

Pinal designs functional proteins from natural-language descriptions

The 16-billion-parameter Pinal model generated protein candidates from plain-language function descriptions. In four reported design tasks—fluorescent protein, PET hydrolase, alcohol dehydrogenase and metabolic H-protein—the selected designs displayed the intended function in laboratory tests; the best H-protein raised product titre 1.7-fold relative to native E. coli H-protein in a reconstituted CO2-fixation pathway.

Technology: Pinal, a 16-billion-parameter protein foundation model with text-to-structure (T2struct) and text-conditioned sequence design (SaProt-T) components

Relevance 67 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: In four reported design tasks—fluorescent protein, PET hydrolase, alcohol dehydrogenase and metabolic H-protein—the selected designs displayed the intended function in laboratory tests; the best H-protein raised product titre 1.7-fold relative to native E. coli H-protein in a reconstituted CO2-fixation pathway.
  • Still unknown: This is a primary-paper proof of concept with selected designs, not a broad hit-rate or industrial-performance demonstration. Experimental denominators vary substantially by task; extended data reports one active PETase among 94 screened and a fluorescent design selected from 29. The H-protein titre comparison was in a reconstituted pathway, not an organism or commercial process. No independent replication or therapeutic result is claimed.
  • AI role: Pinal maps natural-language function descriptions to candidate protein structures and sequences. The team filtered and ranked model outputs, selected candidates for synthesis, and tested intended functions. The paper reports lab-observed fluorescence, PET-hydrolysis/esterase activity, alcohol-dehydrogenase catalysis and H-protein activity; the latter improved titre in a reconstituted multi-enzyme pathway.

Evidence sources: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-11205-z

16 Oct 2026Google Research

AI agents in the lab: aptamers and proteins designed in a closed loop

AI agents designed DNA aptamers and protein binders, which were then tested in the lab over multiple rounds. Target success rates reached 33.5% for myoglobin and 42.1% for α-thrombin (98.5% specificity). The post summarizes three arXiv papers whose peer review is unconfirmed; it also reports initial failures.

Technology: AI agents with laboratory robots

Relevance 67 biology Original source Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: blog. Paper: not linked in this catalog; this does not mean no paper exists.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Preprint
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: Agents ran three design-test-iterate rounds of DNA aptamers against myoglobin and alpha-thrombin in the lab, reaching 33.5% and 42.1% hit rates with 98.5% specificity for thrombin; protein binders against FAP were reported at single-digit nanomolar affinity.
  • Still unknown: Peer review of the cited arXiv papers is unconfirmed; there are two protein targets and three rounds; initial rounds failed, and protein expression adds steps to each loop.
  • AI role: Agents designed and revised sequences across rounds and adjusted design rules after reading wet-lab data, under human oversight of protocols and robot runs.

Evidence sources: https://research.google/blog/grounding-ai-agents-in-biological-reality-building-blocks-for-autonomous-wet-labs/

15 Oct 2026Google DeepMind

Searching for antimicrobial peptides with Google Antigravity

An agentic workflow searched UniProt for antimicrobial candidates in Streptococcus pseudopneumoniae and found a 97-amino-acid bacteriocin with no homologues among manually reviewed Swiss-Prot proteins. This is a computational candidate now undergoing laboratory validation; no potency or toxicity data are available.

Technology: Google Antigravity (agentic platform)

  • Still unknown: No potency or toxicity results exist; the wet-lab assays outcomes are unknown; the session covered a narrow sequence window in one species.

8 Oct 2026Ludwig Maximilian University of Munich; Helmholtz Munich, Max Planck Institute of Biochemistry and Technical University of Munich

SPARCS screen identifies a Golgi-pH regulator of STING signaling

A genome-wide imaging and CRISPR screen of about 70 million cells identified GPR89A and GPR89B (encoding GPHR) as important for maintaining Golgi acidity and early STING trafficking and activation. The study also found a previously undescribed autophagy phenotype associated with EI24 and linked it to altered autophagosome formation.

Technology: SPARCS platform with machine-learning image analysis

  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: A genome-wide imaging and CRISPR screen of about 70 million cells identified GPR89A and GPR89B (encoding GPHR) as important for maintaining Golgi acidity and early STING trafficking and activation.
  • Still unknown: The study used pools of at least 500 excised cells for sequencing, so it could not map individual images to individual guide RNAs. The paper describes GPHR as a regulator of STING trafficking and signaling; therapeutic implications remain untested.
  • AI role: Machine-learning image analysis classified phenotypes in genome-wide CRISPR screens; SPARCS recovered known autophagy genes and surfaced EI24 and GPHR-linked findings. Follow-up molecular profiling and experiments by the researchers examined these hits.

30 Sep 2026Google DeepMind

SynthID Bio: watermarking for proteins and synthetic structures

Methods insert a detectable signal into protein sequences and predicted structures. For binders designed against three targets (VEGF-A, SARS-CoV-2 RBD, and PD-L1), watermarked designs matched the others in success rate and affinity. This is a proof of concept that needs to be made more robust against tampering.

Technology: SynthID Bio, AlphaFold 3 (optimized component)

Family AlphaFold 3: profile · models page

Source type: blog. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Announcement only
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: Wet-lab tests on three targets (VEGF-A, SARS-CoV-2 RBD, PD-L1), with Adaptyv Bio helping, showed watermarked designs matched unwatermarked ones in hit rate, affinity and diversity; the phage was tested only in bacterial cultures.
  • Still unknown: The watermark must become more robust against deliberate tampering, the bacteriophage work is early-stage, and the post says no single biosecurity measure is a silver bullet.
  • AI role: SynthID Bio guided amino acid choice in a ProteinMPNN variant, adjusted predicted atomic coordinates, fine-tuned part of AlphaFold 3, and was integrated into Evo 2 to watermark a designed phage genome.

Evidence sources: https://deepmind.google/blog/introducing-synthid-bio/

29 Sep 2026Microsoft Research; Broad Institute of Harvard and MIT

Quine ranks compounds that shift pancreatic cancer cells toward a basal state

Microsoft's Quine, a multimodal biology world model, prioritized thousands of compounds for shifting tumor cell states, and a handful were tested in pancreatic ductal adenocarcinoma cell lines. The top-ranked compounds produced the largest classical-to-basal shifts, as validated by wet-lab experiments, and some moved cells toward a distinct third phenotype. The post gives no quantitative wet-lab figures and links no paper, and it measures cell-state changes rather than clinical efficacy.

Technology: Quine, multimodal biology world model with interactive harness

23 Sep 2026Anthropic

Claude identifies a new enzyme system: array-associated reverse transcriptases

About 950 Claude agents searched a DNA-sequence database for 21 hours (210 million tokens) and flagged an uncharacterized enzyme system, found mostly in bacteriophages, with a DNA repeat array similar to CRISPR. Scientists performed the laboratory work; the function remains unknown, and the work is a preprint, not a peer-reviewed publication.

Technology: Claude (agents)

Family Claude: profile · models page

  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: Human scientists expressed the protein in laboratory strains and characterized it biochemically and structurally; early experiments showed the array is expressed as distinct short RNAs.
  • Still unknown: The primary function of the enzyme system is unknown; findings are preliminary, and most Claude-proposed candidates are eliminated in follow-up review.
  • AI role: About 950 agents used 210 million tokens over 21 hours to search DNA sequences, narrowing about 3,500 candidate systems to 20; one agent noticed the repeat array and filed a report.

16 Sep 2026Stanford University; collaborating institutions

Paper2Agent integrates perturbation datasets to prioritize GPR137 at a psoriasis risk locus

Paper2Agent-generated agents used AlphaGenome to prioritize GPR137 as the top predicted gene affected by psoriasis-associated variant rs887314 in CD4+ T cells, then integrated that prediction with existing MPRA-coupled scCRISPRi and CD4+ T-cell Perturb-seq data. A cross-screen correlation of gene-expression signatures supported GPR137 as the probable causal gene under stimulated conditions (Spearman rho 0.613 at 8 h and 0.630 at 48 h; FDR < 0.05), but not at rest. The paper presents this as activation-dependent computational evidence.

Technology: Paper2Agent-generated AlphaGenome, MPRA-coupled scCRISPRi and CD4+ T-cell Perturb-seq agents, coordinated through an AI co-scientist

Family AI Co-Scientist: profile · models page

Relevance 53 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Paper2Agent-generated agents used AlphaGenome to prioritize GPR137 as the top predicted gene affected by psoriasis-associated variant rs887314 in CD4+ T cells, then integrated that prediction with existing MPRA-coupled scCRISPRi and CD4+ T-cell Perturb-seq data.
  • Still unknown: The analysis reuses existing published datasets and does not report new wet-lab experiments for this psoriasis case. AlphaGenome supplied a model prediction; cross-dataset signature correlation supports, but does not establish, causal biology. The GPR137 concordance was significant only after stimulation, and the authors describe it as probable/activation-dependent. A human researcher selected which AI-proposed strategy to run. Primary paper source checked; no independent replication assessed.
  • AI role: The AlphaGenome paper agent scored and ranked candidate genes at rs887314. Under human-in-the-loop supervision, the AI co-scientist inspected two other paper agents' datasets, proposed ten validation strategies, and the researcher selected signature-correlation analysis. The resulting cross-screen, cross-modality analysis found significant concordance for GPR137 only under stimulated conditions.

Evidence sources: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-026-11044-y

8 Sep 2026Google DeepMind

AlphaGenome Atlas: predictive map of human DNA variation

A precomputed database of predicted molecular effects for about 9 billion possible single-letter changes in the human genome, generated by AlphaGenome. Researchers can access it free through a portal and API. The predictions are a research tool, do not replace experiments, and are not validated for clinical use.

Technology: AlphaGenome, AlphaGenome Variant Impact (AVI)

Family AlphaGenome: profile · models page

4 Sep 2026Goethe University Frankfurt

YOLO detector tracks endangered European hamster activity in camera traps

A YOLO object-detection model trained on 2023 camera-trap videos detected European hamsters with an F1-score of 0.93 and accuracy of 0.93. Its activity profile matched human annotation and showed peak activity at dusk and a secondary peak before sunrise. The evidence covers one season at one monitoring setup, so generalization to other sites is untested.

Technology: YOLO object detection (deep learning)

31 Aug 2026Central China Normal University, State Key Laboratory of Green Pesticide

ESM-FEP combines protein-language-model fitness screening and free-energy calculations to engineer herbicide-detoxifying maize dioxygenase

ESM-FEP was applied to Zea mays dioxygenase ZmHSL1B and nominated a quadruple mutant (Q140H/Y205F/L332R/K336F) with approximately sevenfold higher catalytic efficiency for mesotrione detoxification than wild type, measured by in-vitro assays and kinetic analysis. Arabidopsis thaliana expressing the engineered variant showed enhanced herbicide tolerance in the reported transgenic tests.

Technology: ESM-FEP (fine-tuned ESM-2 protein language model plus alchemical free-energy perturbation)

Family ESM / ESMFold: profile · models page

  • Tested: ESM-FEP was applied to Zea mays dioxygenase ZmHSL1B and nominated a quadruple mutant (Q140H/Y205F/L332R/K336F) with approximately sevenfold higher catalytic efficiency for mesotrione detoxification than wild type, measured by in-vitro assays and kinetic analysis.
  • Still unknown: Reported results concern one maize dioxygenase and mesotrione in specified in-vitro and Arabidopsis tests. They do not establish crop-field performance, commercial efficacy, or generality across herbicides/enzymes. Primary PubMed abstract and publisher citation metadata checked; no independent replication assessed.
  • AI role: Fine-tuned ESM-2 screened saturation-mutagenesis variants for retained global protein fitness; free-energy perturbation then estimated substrate-binding effects and prioritized mutants. The selected M5 mutant was assayed in vitro and in transgenic Arabidopsis by the researchers.

Evidence sources: https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/42675858/

25 Aug 2026Research Institute of Molecular Pathology (IMP), Vienna BioCenter; University of California, Irvine; Vienna BioCenter PhD Program, University of Vienna and Medical University of Vienna

Deep-learning-designed enhancers drive target-tissue reporter activity in mouse embryos

Researchers trained convolutional neural networks on E11.5 mouse heart, limb and midbrain chromatin-accessibility data, then fine-tuned the models using validated human and mouse enhancers and used model-guided sequence optimization to design 15 synthetic enhancers. In vivo transgenic reporter assays found all 15 reproducibly active in their intended tissue. Four of five heart designs and four of five CNS designs were exclusive to the target tissue; all five limb designs were strongly active in limb and had weaker activity in other TWIST1-expressing mesenchymal tissues.

Technology: Convolutional neural networks for sequence-to-accessibility and sequence-to-activity prediction, transfer learning, and Ledidi gradient-based sequence design

6 Aug 2026University of Chicago

Machine-learning-guided CsrA homolog screens reveal divergent bacterial regulatory activities

A Swarm-seq assay tested more than 500 bacterial CsrA homologs for regulation of Bacillus subtilis swarming and found two functional classes: several homologs strongly inhibited swarming while others failed despite conserved sequence and canonical GGA motifs in the hag transcript. A random-forest model trained on these assays predicted homolog activity across thousands of sequences; experimental testing supported predicted Class I regulatory activity for Bdellovibrio bacteriovorus CsrA and Pseudomonas putida RsmA. The findings indicate sequence conservation and the canonical RNA motif do not alone determine these tested regulatory effects.

Technology: Random-forest machine-learning classifier trained on Swarm-seq functional assay data

28 Jul 2026University of Tübingen; Université Claude Bernard Lyon 1 and collaborating institutions

Machine learning prioritizes a protective Arabidopsis leaf fungus that suppresses oomycete infection

Four classifiers (random forest, support-vector machine, logistic regression and multilayer perceptron) used six years of natural Arabidopsis leaf-microbiome data to prioritize taxa associated with Albugo infection status. Follow-up plant assays found that selected health-associated microbes reduced infection; Cystofilobasidium macerans OTU126 gave the strongest individual protection, reducing Albugo infection by an average of 73%. In a 15-member synthetic leaf community, adding this fungus increased the reported infection reduction to 40.25%, compared with 19.36% for the community alone.

Technology: Random forest, support-vector machine, logistic regression and multilayer perceptron classifiers with cross-validated feature selection

18 Jul 2026Duke University

Deep-learning splice prediction links a PARP2 variant to altered protein isoforms and influenza susceptibility

By combining single-cell splicing-QTL analysis during influenza A infection with SpliceAI/Pangolin annotation and statistical fine-mapping, researchers prioritized causal splice variants. They experimentally characterized rs2297616 in PARP2: its A allele creates an alternative 5′ splice donor, adding 39 nucleotides and producing a protein isoform 13 amino acids longer. CRISPR-edited A549 cells showed the predicted splice and protein changes; the A-allele edited cells also had higher influenza A infection than G-allele cells. The variant was associated with higher viral burden in a panel of 96 lymphoblastoid cell lines.

Technology: SpliceAI and Pangolin deep-learning splice-effect predictors, integrated with single-cell scHi-HOST sQTL mapping and SuSiE statistical fine-mapping

22 Jun 2026Tsinghua University; Tianjin University; Inner Mongolia University; Zhejiang Institute of Tianjin University

Few-shot protein-language-model design yields high-output bicyclogermacrene synthase variants

After experimentally screening activity-enhancing single mutants of LdTPS5, the researchers used PIFS-PLM, a few-shot model that selects features from protein-language-model embeddings using active-site and conserved-residue information, to propose mutation combinations. All 10 tested model-predicted combinations were active; nine produced more than 100 mg/L bicyclogermacrene in yeast, and the top variant produced 213.59 mg/L versus 2.93 mg/L for wild type (about 73-fold higher yield). A related selectivity-optimized combination increased bicyclogermacrene selectivity by 15%.

Technology: PIFS-PLM (Physics-Inspired Feature Selection of Protein Language Models), using ESM-2 embeddings with a few-shot mutation-combination classifier

12 May 2026ETH Zurich; Weizmann Institute of Science; University Children’s Hospital Zurich and University of Zurich

Machine-learning metabolomics identifies widespread promiscuous activities in Escherichia coli enzymes

In vitro metabolomics of 667 purified E. coli enzymes, analyzed with a machine-learning-derived multivariate classifier, indicated previously unrecognized promiscuous activities in 11% of enzymes tested. Follow-up reconstruction assigned 22 reaction stoichiometries to 11 enzymes, with four reactions experimentally validated; examples include expanded nucleotide-substrate use by DeoA, cofactor and sugar promiscuity in NanK, and FMN dephosphorylation by CobC. Follow-up assays also found that pyrimidine-specific ribonucleoside hydrolase RihB acted on thymidine, guanosine and deoxyuridine, further extending its measured substrate range.

Technology: Machine-learning-derived multivariate classifier applied to enzyme metabolomics ion-trace data

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: In vitro metabolomics of 667 purified E. coli enzymes, analyzed with a machine-learning-derived multivariate classifier, indicated previously unrecognized promiscuous activities in 11% of enzymes tested.
  • Still unknown: The classifier operated at a 33% false-discovery rate; the 135 ion traces were putatively annotated, and only four of 22 reconstructed reaction stoichiometries were experimentally validated. Therefore the full 11% result is a set of candidate activities, not 11% independently confirmed enzyme functions. The RihB activities were prompted by a low-confidence potassium-thymidine-adduct trace and then tested directly with purified enzyme; they should be distinguished from the classifier’s higher-confidence hit set.
  • AI role: A machine-learning-derived multivariate classifier, with a reported false-discovery rate of 33%, detected unexpected changes in metabolite ion traces in the enzyme assays and helped nominate candidate promiscuous activities. The researchers then reconstructed candidate reaction stoichiometries and experimentally validated four reactions.

30 Apr 2026Columbia University; Massachusetts Institute of Technology and Harvard University

AI-guided design enables E. coli ribosomal proteins without isoleucine

Researchers used computational protein design and generative AI to recode ribosomal proteins in E. coli so they function without isoleucine, then assembled an engineered strain that remained viable and genomically stable over more than 450 laboratory generations. The result shows a reduced amino-acid alphabet can support core translation machinery in this bacterium; the organism still uses isoleucine elsewhere.

Technology: Protein design models including ESM2, MSATr, ProteinMPNN and AfDesign

30 Apr 2026Washington University School of Medicine; University of Miami

CellOracle-guided pancreatic islet differentiation experiments reveal STAT1 and ZEB1 lineage roles

A digital model built from 400,603 cells across 61 single-cell datasets used CellOracle gene-regulatory-network inference and in-silico transcription-factor perturbations to nominate regulators of stem-cell-derived human islet differentiation. CRISPRi experiments supported a previously unreported role for STAT1 in exocrine specification: transient STAT1 knockdown reduced exocrine markers, and JAK–STAT inhibition preserved beta-cell markers and improved glucose-stimulated insulin secretion. CRISPRi also supported a stage-dependent role for ZEB1, whose early knockdown impaired endocrine specification and later knockdown reduced off-target serotonergic cells while increasing beta-cell proportion. The date is the first preprint posting; this entry summarizes the later version reviewed in the research record, which reports the specific STAT1 and ZEB1 experiments.

Technology: CellOracle v0.12.0 within a digital model integrating single-cell transcriptomic and chromatin-accessibility data

28 Mar 2026KAIST; University of Washington Institute for Protein Design

AI-designed protein family binds and senses small molecules, including cortisol

The study combines trRosetta-based scaffold hallucination and Rosetta design with ProteinMPNN/LigandMPNN sequence design and AlphaFold structural filtering to create NTF2-like binders for six small-molecule targets. An optimized cortisol binder had a measured dissociation constant of 68 nM. A purified-protein, split-luciferase cortisol sensor showed an estimated response EC50 of 72 nM; this is a sensor response measurement, not the binding-affinity value or a clinical detection limit. The sensor also responded to cortisone and 17-alpha-hydroxyprogesterone, so specificity needs improvement. The March 2026 peer-reviewed paper is the source of record.

Technology: trRosetta-based hallucination; Rosetta; ProteinMPNN/LigandMPNN sequence design; AlphaFold structural filtering

24 Feb 2026Université libre de Bruxelles; Hôpital Erasme; Milieu Intérieur cohort collaborators

Machine-learning analysis separates genetic from environmental drivers of stimulated cytokine responses in healthy adults

In 406 healthy GEOCODE cohort participants, whole-blood cytokine responses to Toll-like-receptor (TLR) and T-cell-receptor (TCR) stimulation varied across people, including high- and low-IL-6-response profiles associated with monocyte counts, sex and sampling season. Feature-importance analysis of machine-learning models found TCR-induced cytokine predictions were mainly driven by genetic features, whereas TLR-induced predictions were driven more by environmental and biological features. Combining genetic with biological and environmental data improved prediction by about 0.2 on average, and the reported findings were evaluated in the independent Milieu Intérieur cohort.

Technology: Machine-learning models including random forest, gradient-boosted trees, artificial neural networks and elastic net; random forest feature-importance analysis

  • Tested: In 406 healthy GEOCODE cohort participants, whole-blood cytokine responses to Toll-like-receptor (TLR) and T-cell-receptor (TCR) stimulation varied across people, including high- and low-IL-6-response profiles associated with monocyte counts, sex and sampling season.

23 Dec 2025Tongji Hospital, Tongji Medical College, Huazhong University of Science and Technology

Machine-learning metabolomics prioritizes succinate in sepsis-associated encephalopathy

In plasma samples from 29 controls, 32 sepsis patients and 27 patients with sepsis-associated encephalopathy (SAE), LC–MS/MS plus machine-learning feature selection identified a 12-metabolite profile. Succinate rose stepwise from controls to sepsis to SAE, ranked among the five leading XGBoost features, and correlated with APACHE II, SOFA and lactate. In a cecal-ligation-and-puncture mouse model, dietary succinate supplementation before sepsis worsened cognitive-test performance, neuronal injury and microglial activation; succinate also increased inflammatory gene expression in cultured microglia.

Technology: Random forest and support-vector-machine feature ranking with XGBoost classification

3 Dec 2025TU Graz; and University of Graz (Oberdorfer group)

Riff-Diff: computational enzyme design by catalytic motif scaffolding

Riff-Diff places catalytic motifs around an active centre and uses RFdiffusion to generate the full protein; 35 tested sequences yielded active enzymes for several reaction types, reported as significantly faster than earlier computational designs. Angstrom-level accuracy was checked against experimentally determined high-resolution structures, and almost all designs kept their fold up to about 90 degrees Celsius. Figures come from university press coverage; the Nature paper itself was not read directly.

1 Dec 2025Shanghai Institute of Organic Chemistry, Chinese Academy of Sciences; Shanghai Artificial Intelligence Laboratory; collaborating Chinese research institutions

AI-guided subtilisin variants improve aqueous amide formation

The study reports that wild-type subtilisin BPN′ directly catalyzed condensation of free carboxylic acids and amines in aqueous PBS (pH 8, 40 °C), forming a peptide-bond amide without stoichiometric coupling reagents. The authors combined AI-guided mutation ranking with a FRET assay, reducing 7,999 in-silico variants at three active-site positions to five experimental candidates. The top triple mutant N155G/N218D/M222Q had reported relative activity of 753 ± 14% of wild type and also coupled biotin to a peptide fragment in water, extending beyond peptide–peptide ligation.

Technology: AI-guided enzyme mutation prediction combining binding-free-energy analysis of active-site substitutions with an experimental FRET-based rapid product-detection platform

19 Nov 2025Arc Institute; Stanford University

Evo genomic language model designs functional toxin-antitoxin and anti-CRISPR systems

Evo used genomic context to generate functional bacterial defence-system components. The paper reports an AI-generated toxin and low-sequence-similarity antitoxins that neutralized it in growth assays, including antitoxins that also acted against multiple natural toxins; it also reports five generated anti-CRISPR proteins that protected bacteria against Cas9 activity in cell and phage assays. Two generated anti-CRISPR proteins lacked significant sequence similarity to known proteins. These experiments demonstrate function-guided genomic design beyond simple copying of natural sequences.

  • AI role: Researchers prompted Evo 1.5 with genomic contexts that associate functionally related bacterial genes and used it to generate candidate toxin-antitoxin and anti-CRISPR sequences. Candidates were filtered computationally, synthesized/expressed and tested in bacterial growth, Cas9-protection and phage assays. AI generated candidates; the biological activity was established experimentally.

Evidence sources: https://www.nature.com/articles/s41586-025-09749-7.pdf

6 Oct 2025Evozyne; NVIDIA

ProT-VAE designs synthetic enzymes with measured activity and high-temperature stability

The transformer–variational-autoencoder ProT-VAE generated synthetic enzyme sequences that were tested experimentally. Among 190 designed human phenylalanine-hydroxylase sequences, 69 had measurable activity and 19 exceeded wild-type activity, with the best at 2.5× wild type. A designed γ-carbonic anhydrase had a measured melting temperature of 116 °C (61 °C above the most thermostable sequence previously reported by the authors) and remained active in 23% methyl diethanolamine at pH 11.25 and 93 °C.

Technology: Protein Transformer Variational AutoEncoder (ProT-VAE), using ProtT5nv transformer representations and a family-specific variational autoencoder

18 Aug 2025Agricultural Genomics Institute at Shenzhen, Chinese Academy of Agricultural Sciences

AlphaCD maps experimentally measured catalytic properties across cytidine deaminases and supports a high-fidelity editor

The researchers measured catalytic efficiency, editing windows, motif preferences and off-target activity for 1,100 APOBEC-like cytidine deaminases fused to nCas9 in HEK293T cells, then trained AlphaCD to predict these properties. They applied it to 21,335 UniProt deaminases and experimentally checked a 28-protein subsample. As an application, the team used AlphaCD to identify a high-efficiency deaminase and alanine scanning to produce the E100A variant, which retained editing activity while reducing off-target activity; cell experiments tested editing at endogenous loci and a PCSK9 splice site.

Technology: AlphaCD, a machine-learning model trained on sequence, predicted structure and related protein features

23 Jul 2025Sanquin Blood Supply Foundation; University of Amsterdam; Amsterdam UMC; Oncode Institute

SONAR identifies 3′UTR sequence features that raise T-cell receptor protein expression and CD8-cell killing

The XGBoost-based SONAR pipeline modeled protein abundance from thousands of sequence features across human immune cells and cell lines, identifying cell-type- and activation-dependent regulatory patterns. Reporter assays found that CT-rich 3′UTR motifs increased protein output while AG-rich motifs were generally repressive. SONAR-informed synthetic 3′UTRs raised CDK4-specific T-cell-receptor protein without increasing its mRNA; engineered CD8+ T cells carrying the CT-rich constructs killed CDK4-expressing melanoma cells more effectively in a coculture assay.

Technology: SONAR, an XGBoost machine-learning pipeline using thousands of sequence features to predict protein abundance

Machine learning and language models choosing variants, with a biofoundry running build and test, raised Arabidopsis halide methyltransferase substrate preference 90-fold (16-fold ethyltransferase activity) and Yersinia mollaretii phytase activity 26-fold at neutral pH, in four rounds over four weeks and under 500 variants per enzyme. 25 Jun 2025Google DeepMind

AlphaGenome: predicting the effects of DNA variants

The model reads sequences up to 1 million base pairs and predicts thousands of gene-regulation properties at single-letter resolution. It outperformed the best external models in 22 of 24 sequence evaluations and matched or outperformed the best in 24 of 26 variant evaluations. Stated limits: distant regulatory elements; not validated for personal genomes or clinical use.

13 May 2025German Cancer Research Center (DKFZ); European Molecular Biology Laboratory (EMBL); Wellcome Sanger Institute

Deep learning identifies MYT1L as a regulator that restricts glioblastoma plasticity

scDORI inferred enhancer-linked regulatory networks from single-cell multiome data and prioritized regulators for perturbation. MYT1L overexpression restricted alternative cell states and reduced tumor growth and invasion in mice; loss of MYT1L restored malignant features. This is preclinical evidence, not an established treatment. The result was public in a May 13, 2025 preprint; the peer-reviewed paper appeared November 4, 2026.

8 May 2025Centre for Genomic Regulation; and Barcelona Collaboratorium

AI-designed enhancers control gene expression in healthy mammalian cells

CRG researchers measured more than 64,000 synthetic enhancers across seven stages of blood-cell development and found that enhancer combinations can activate genes in one cell type but repress them in another. The AI model used those measurements to design previously unseen regulatory DNA sequences that drove the intended cell-selective reporter expression in mouse blood cells.

Technology: Machine-learning enhancer design model developed by Lars Velten's group

23 Apr 2025University of California San Diego; collaborating biomedical research institutions

AlphaFold structure analysis helps reveal PHGDH transcriptional activity linked to Alzheimer’s amyloid pathology

AlphaFold-based structural analysis highlighted a helix-helix-turn-helix subdomain in PHGDH that resembles a TALE homeodomain, prompting tests of a previously unrecognized DNA-binding and transcriptional function. Experiments supported PHGDH binding to promoters and activation of IKKα and HMGB1 in astrocytes, independently of its metabolic enzyme activity. In mouse models and human brain organoids, PHGDH expression promoted amyloid pathology; a blood-brain-barrier-permeable inhibitor of its transcriptional function reduced amyloid pathology and improved measured behavioral deficits in mice.

Technology: AlphaFold structure prediction, followed by structural comparison of the predicted PHGDH fold

MICrONS: a map of one cubic millimeter of mouse visual cortex

A map with over half a billion connections and about 75,000 reconstructed neurons from roughly 28,000 sections, produced over nine years by more than 150 researchers. 6 Apr 2025Westlake University; and collaborating institutions

ESM-Ezy mining identifies multicopper oxidases and asparaginases with experimentally improved catalytic properties

The ESM-1b semantic-embedding mining strategy nominated low-sequence-similarity enzyme candidates. In the reported experimental assessment, 44% of tested multicopper oxidases outperformed their query enzymes in at least one measured property (including catalytic efficiency, heat/solvent tolerance or pH stability); 51% were reported to excel in environmental-remediation applications. Among mined L-asparaginases, 40% had higher specific activity and catalytic efficiency than query enzymes.

Technology: ESM-Ezy, using ESM-1b protein-language-model embeddings and semantic-space similarity

20 Mar 2025Shandong University; Shanghai Zelixir Biotech; Shenzhen Institute of Advanced Technology, Chinese Academy of Sciences

CataPro-assisted enzyme mining identifies a high-activity Sphingobium enzyme for vanillin production

CataPro, a deep-learning model using pretrained protein-language representations and molecular fingerprints to predict enzyme kinetics, was combined with sequence and structure-based filtering to screen candidate enzymes for converting 4-vinylguaiacol to vanillin. The team experimentally identified Sphingobium sp. CSO (SsCSO), whose measured activity was 19.53 times that of the initial enzyme CSO2, and then used CataPro-guided engineering to increase SsCSO activity a further 3.34-fold.

TeleProt-guided engineering yields a nuclease variant with improved neutral-pH biofilm degradation

A machine-learning-guided design and high-throughput screening campaign produced a Bacillus licheniformis NucB variant with 19-fold higher measured specific activity than wild type at pH 7. At 1 μM in a laboratory biofilm assay at neutral pH, the selected variant degraded 71% ± 2% of the biofilm versus 13% ± 3% for wild-type NucB.

11 Mar 2025Carleton University; Ben-Gurion University of the Negev

Machine learning and peptide-array experiments reveal new substrates for protein-modifying enzymes

A hybrid workflow coupled machine-learning models with enzyme-treated peptide arrays to predict previously unexplored protein post-translational-modification sites. Experimental validation confirmed 37–43% of proposed sites across SET8 and SIRT1–7; mass spectrometry confirmed dynamic methylation for several SET8 substrates and deacetylation at 64 unique SIRT2 sites. The work also found altered SET8 substrate networks associated with breast-cancer missense mutations.

Technology: Enzyme-specific ML-hybrid substrate prediction models trained from high-throughput peptide-array assays

AI-guided promoter editing raises maize ZmVTE4 expression and kernel alpha-tocopherol

Basenji2-based deep-learning models and editing-plasticity analysis guided CRISPR editing of maize ZmVTE4, a gene involved in vitamin E biosynthesis. Researchers isolated a previously unobserved four-base deletion in the gene’s 5′ untranslated region; the edited allele significantly increased ZmVTE4 expression and α-tocopherol content in kernels. A separate promoter-variant assay supported the four-base deletion, rather than a co-occurring 82-base deletion, as the key change associated with increased reporter activity.

Technology: Basenji2-long and Basenji2-3K-NAM sequence-to-expression deep-learning models with editing-plasticity analysis

5 Mar 2025University of Oxford; 119-author international team

Canopy traits across tropical forests mapped with deep learning and satellite data

Field data from more than 1,800 vegetation plots, combined with satellite, terrain, climate and soil data and convolutional neural networks, predicted variation in 13 tree functional traits. Tropical American forests show about 40% greater functional richness than African and Asian forests, and African forests show 32% higher functional divergence than American forests. The authors caution that accuracy depends on data quality and coverage, and that AI should support rather than replace field sampling.

Technology: Convolutional neural networks combined with satellite spectral data

BioEmu-1: ensembles of protein structures with deep learning

Ridge-regression ML guided enzyme variants that produced nine small-molecule pharmaceuticals with activity 1.6- to 42-fold above the parent; initial substrate-promiscuity assays found McbA could make 11 pharmaceutical compounds plus hybrid molecules. 16 Jan 2025California Institute of Technology; University of California, Berkeley; Elegen Corp

Active learning identifies a protoglobin variant with 99% yield and cis-selective cyclopropanation

An iterative machine-learning campaign engineered the protoglobin from Pyrobaculum arsenaticum (ParPgb) for non-native cyclopropanation of 4-vinylanisole. After two model-guided rounds following an initial library screen, variant MPFDY gave 99% total cyclopropane yield and a 14:1 cis:trans ratio in the reported assay, compared with a parent variant that produced about 40% yield and favored trans product. The result demonstrates a specific sequence combination with improved yield and stereoselectivity in this enzyme scaffold.

Technology: ALDE (Active Learning-assisted Directed Evolution), using a bootstrapped deep-neural-network ensemble and Thompson sampling

8 Jan 2025Columbia University; Mohamed bin Zayed University of Artificial Intelligence; collaborating institutions

GET and interaction assays link a leukemia-risk PAX5 variant to stronger NR2C2 binding

The General Expression Transformer (GET) model inferred cell-type-specific transcription-factor motif interactions from regulatory data and nominated a lymphocyte-specific interaction between PAX5 and nuclear-receptor-family factors. AlphaFold multimer modeling and PAX5 proximity-labeling in a B-ALL cell line supported interaction with NR2C2; the leukemia-associated PAX5 G183S variant showed increased PAX5–NR2C2 proximity signal. The variant-specific expression profile was enriched for predicted NR/3 and shared PAX5/NR/3 target genes, suggesting a possible regulatory mechanism for the germline risk variant.

Technology: GET (General Expression Transformer) foundation model for cell-type-specific regulatory grammar; AlphaFold2/AlphaFold Multimer for structural hypothesis assessment

11 Dec 2024Max Planck Institute of Molecular Cell Biology and Genetics; Center for Systems Biology Dresden; TU Dresden Cluster of Excellence Physics of Life

PICNIC machine learning predictions revealed previously poorly characterized human proteins in cellular condensates

The PICNIC gradient-boosting model ranked proteins for membership in biomolecular condensates using sequence-pattern and AlphaFold2-derived structural features. To test its predictions, researchers expressed 24 poorly characterized human proteins in U2OS cells: 21 formed mesoscale fluorescent foci, and 18 met the paper’s high-confidence condensate threshold. The experiments mapped examples to cytoplasmic bodies, nuclear bodies, nucleoli or P-bodies; for example, TYW5 and RAD51AP1 showed distinct localization patterns. Across the tested set, the authors found condensate localization was not limited to highly disordered proteins.

Technology: PICNIC, a gradient-boosting machine-learning classifier using protein sequence co-occurrence patterns, sequence complexity/disorder features and AlphaFold2-derived structural features

9 Dec 2024Northeast Normal University; Chinese Academy of Sciences; University of Exeter; John Innes Centre; Alan Turing Institute

PlantRNA-FM identifies translation-associated RNA motifs and structure motifs in plants

The PlantRNA-FM model, pretrained on sequence and predicted RNA structure from 1,124 plant transcriptomes, identified 5′-UTR motifs associated with translation efficiency across plant transcriptomes. Reporter assays in plants tested selected predictions: mutating a high-translation-associated RNA secondary-structure motif reduced measured translation efficiency by up to 5.3-fold, while disrupting a model-prioritized low-translation-associated RNA G-quadruplex motif increased it by up to 5.8-fold. The paper reports the rG4 motifs as translation-repressive, consistent with prior evidence on individual rG4s; the transcriptome-wide model prioritization and tested instances are the scoped findings here.

Technology: PlantRNA-FM, an interpretable RNA foundation model integrating RNA sequence and predicted structure

EphcCasλ activity and PAM characterization are in-vitro findings. The article has a publisher-listed author correction dated 2025-01-09; this record relies on the version-of-record text and retains limited scope.
  • AI role: CHOOSER mined candidate proteins adjacent to CRISPR arrays using learned sequence representations rather than alignment-only detection and prioritized Cas12 homologs by predicted pre-crRNA processing. The study reported 11 Casλ homologs and experimentally tested selected predictions; purified EphcCasλ was validated for self-processing pre-crRNA, DNA cleavage and trans-cleavage.

2 Nov 2024Broad Institute of MIT and Harvard; collaborating institutions

HiDDEN finds malignant-like cell signals in early multiple-myeloma precursor samples

HiDDEN refines sample-level case labels at single-cell resolution. In human bone-marrow single-cell RNA-seq data, its labels tracked manual neoplastic-cell annotations in mixed precursor samples and inferred malignant-like cells in three low-purity MGUS samples that the original manual analysis had labeled as containing only healthy cells. The inferred cells were enriched for previously described malignancy-associated gene signatures. In a separate mouse demyelination dataset, HiDDEN identified an early endothelial subpopulation with expression changes associated with angiogenesis, blood–brain-barrier breakdown and extracellular-matrix responses.

Technology: HiDDEN, a machine-learning framework for refining disease/condition labels at single-cell resolution

25 Oct 2024Dana-Farber Cancer Institute; Broad Institute of MIT and Harvard

Transcriptome-based machine learning reveals an MCL1 dependency in alveolar soft part sarcoma

A support-vector-regression model trained to infer gene dependencies from RNA-sequencing profiles predicted selective MCL1 dependence in alveolar soft part sarcoma (ASPS), distinct from TFE3-translocation renal cell carcinoma. The authors tested the prediction with CRISPR knockout and the MCL1 inhibitor murizatoclax in two ASPS cell lines; the reported viability effects were greater in ASPS than comparator tRCC lines.

23 Oct 2024Broad Institute of MIT and Harvard; The Jackson Laboratory; Yale University

Machine-guided synthetic regulatory DNA drives cell-type-specific expression across cell lines and vertebrates

The CODA platform used the Malinois deep-learning model and sequence-search algorithms to design synthetic cis-regulatory elements (CREs). In massively parallel reporter assays, 94.1% of the synthetic sequences met the authors’ cell-type-specificity criterion, versus 73.6% of model-selected natural sequences and 40.6% of chromatin-mark-selected natural sequences; synthetic sequences also had higher measured target/off-target separation across K562, HepG2 and SK-N-SH cells. In transgenic zebrafish, two of three liver-directed and two of three neuron-directed CREs showed the intended activity. One neuron-directed sequence, synN1, retained cortical and neuronal expression in mice, including postnatal neocortical layer 6 and subplate neurons.

Technology: CODA (Computational Optimization of DNA Activity) with Malinois deep neural network and AdaLead, simulated annealing, and Fast SeqProp sequence search/design algorithms

11 Oct 2024Gladstone-UCSF Institute of Genomic Immunology; University of California, San Francisco; Johns Hopkins University; Stanford University; University of California, Berkeley

CRISPR perturbation and Bayesian network inference map immune-disease regulatory cascades in primary CD4+ T cells

The study used CRISPR knockouts of 84 genes in primary human CD4+ T cells and a new linear latent causal Bayes (LLCB) network-inference method to estimate 211 regulatory connections. Integrating the inferred network with immune GWAS analyses identified an interconnected module in which KMT2A was inferred upstream of JAK-STAT and IL-2 signaling regulators including IRF4, STAT5A/B and IL2RA, linking an epigenetic regulator to immune-disease-associated pathways.

Technology: Linear latent causal Bayes (LLCB), a Bayesian extension of linear latent causal network inference, applied to CRISPR knockout and RNA-sequencing data

Relevance 60 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: The study used CRISPR knockouts of 84 genes in primary human CD4+ T cells and a new linear latent causal Bayes (LLCB) network-inference method to estimate 211 regulatory connections.
  • Still unknown: The study perturbed 84 selected genes in primary CD4+ T cells from three donors, so this is not a complete T-cell regulatory map. LLCB “direct effects” are statistical effects adjusted for other perturbed genes and are not evidence of physical binding; effects from unperturbed genes can be attributed to modeled edges. The GWAS integration identifies pathway links, not proof that the inferred cascade causes clinical disease. A prior bioRxiv version (10.1101/2023.09.17.557749) is the same study and is not counted separately.
  • AI role: LLCB inferred direct and indirect regulatory relationships from perturbation-associated expression effects across 84 targeted genes. The model-derived network and gene-program analysis, combined with immune GWAS statistics, supported the specific KMT2A-to-JAK-STAT/IL-2 regulatory finding. CRISPR perturbation supplied the experimental perturbation data; the network algorithm converted those measurements into the reported regulatory map.

18 Sep 2024University of Washington Institute for Protein Design; University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center; Broad Institute; Massachusetts General Hospital; University of Connecticut; Seoul National University; collaborating institutions

Deep learning identifies previously unreported protein interactions in human bacterial pathogens

A RoseTTAFold2-Lite pipeline combined residue coevolution, deep-learning complex prediction and AlphaFold modelling to screen protein pairs across 19 human bacterial pathogens. In a validation set selected using the deep-learning pipeline, co-immunoprecipitation detected four of six predicted interactions. Three pairs lacked prior direct interaction evidence: E. coli UbiE–YcaR, P. aeruginosa PA4105–PA4106, and P. aeruginosa WbpG–HisF2. The PA4105–PA4106 structural similarity to proteins in 3-thiaglutamate biosynthesis and the WbpG–HisF2 result provide functional hypotheses, not established native-pathogen mechanisms.

Technology: RoseTTAFold2-Lite integrated with direct coupling analysis and AlphaFold complex modelling

Researchers applied MODIFY, an ensemble model combining protein language models and sequence-density models, to design a diverse high-fitness library for Rhodothermus marinus cytochrome c. Experimental screening identified variant MLYPPT, which catalyzed both non-natural borylation and silylation: reported total turnover numbers were 2,880 and 2,740, with enantiomeric ratios of 94:6 and 97:3, respectively. The same library yielded distinct high-performing variants for each reaction.

  • AI role: The classifier used genomic and protein biochemical features to score candidate EAT genes across eCIS-positive microbial genomes. The model narrowed candidates for functional tests; heterologous expression assays validated four previously uncharacterized toxins, with follow-up active-site mutations and a human-cell signaling assay.

Evidence sources: https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44320-024-00053-6

25 Jul 2024University of Pittsburgh School of Pharmacy; University of Pittsburgh Center for Antibody Therapeutics; University of Pittsburgh Center for AIDS Research

AI-guided HuJ3 mutation improves HIV-1 gp120 binding and neutralization in assays

The IsAb2.0 workflow, combining AlphaFold-Multimer complex modeling with FlexddG mutation scoring, proposed five substitutions to improve the binding of humanized J3 nanobody HuJ3 to HIV-1 gp120. Of these, E44R experimentally improved binding (ELISA EC50 1.696 nM) and neutralization relative to HuJ3; the other proposed substitutions did not consistently improve binding, and T99R failed to express. The gain did not restore neutralization to the parental llama J3 level.

Technology: IsAb2.0 antibody-design protocol integrating AlphaFold-Multimer 2.3/3.0, SnugDock, alanine scanning and FlexddG

AlphaFold2-guided mining identifies context-independent cytidine deaminases for efficient base editing

Researchers used AlphaFold2 to predict structures for 1,483 cytidine deaminases, clustered the predictions and experimentally characterized representatives. 29 May 2024Department of General Surgery, General Hospital of Northern Theater Command, Shenyang, China

Machine-learning analysis linked two sepsis-associated neutrophil clusters to five mouse-validated marker genes

Reanalysis of single-cell profiles from four sepsis patients and two healthy controls identified two neutrophil clusters enriched in the sepsis samples and a set of cluster-associated gene modules. LASSO and seven additional machine-learning classifiers prioritized eight hub genes, including ALPL, CD177, S100A8, S100A9 and STXBP2. Expression analyses in separate public sepsis transcriptome cohorts supported several markers, and qRT-PCR in cecal-ligation/puncture and LPS mouse models found increased neutrophil expression of those five genes. The result links sepsis-associated neutrophil states to a small marker panel, with experimental expression validation in mice.

Technology: LASSO feature selection followed by seven machine-learning classifiers (k-nearest neighbors, linear discriminant analysis, logistic regression, naive Bayes, random forest, recursive partitioning and support-vector machine), combined with hdWGCNA and single-cell transcriptomic analysis

22 May 2024ETH Zurich; University of Basel and University of Regensburg

Active learning identifies a higher-activity streptavidin variant for gold-catalyzed hydroamination

An iterative active-learning campaign engineered a streptavidin artificial metalloenzyme for the known-to-the-authors gold-catalyzed hydroamination of 2-ethynylaniline to indole. Gaussian-process models guided screening across five residues in a 3.2-million-variant sequence space. The final-round library contained 38% variants more active than the prior reference, compared with 3% in the initial library. The leading variant, Sav CTNLV, showed 18-fold higher cell-specific activity than wild-type Sav and threefold higher activity than the previous reference variant in replicate testing.

Technology: Active-learning Gaussian-process regression model with optimized sequence descriptors and experimental-noise estimates

14 May 2024Harvard Medical School; Massachusetts Institute of Technology; collaborating institutions

Machine learning links mycobacterial protein disorder to targeted degradation potential

Researchers combined a genetic chemical-proximity platform and experimental screening of 72 Mycobacterium smegmatis protein candidates with machine learning. A Lasso model trained on measured degradation data identified N-terminal disorder propensity as associated with degradation potential and predicted candidate targets among 348 essential proteins conserved between M. smegmatis and M. tuberculosis. Experimentally induced degradation of selected essential M. smegmatis proteins inhibited bacterial growth and, for some targets, potentiated antimicrobial compounds.

Technology: Lasso regression model trained on sequence and structural protein descriptors, including AlphaFold2 confidence and predicted disorder propensity

OpenCRISPR-1: a genome editor designed by language models

  • Still unknown: The metabolite–receptor screen is computational and many pairs remain predictions. Receptor activation was tested in a cell assay and tau-marker effects in patient-derived neurons; these experiments do not demonstrate clinical benefit, in-vivo efficacy or that altered tau markers translate to disease modification. Mendelian-randomization results and model scores are not proof of causality.
  • AI role: The ML model ranked predicted metabolite–GPCR associations over a large search space; multi-omics and genetic analyses prioritized Alzheimer’s-related receptors and metabolites. These predictions guided follow-up assays, including triacanthine activation of GPR84 and testing agmatine and phenethylamine in patient-derived neurons.

13 Apr 2024University of Parma; University of Padua; Sapienza University of Rome; CNR Institute of Molecular Biology and Pathology

Structure-based virtual screening identifies mammalian enzymes catalyzing the second step of carnitine biosynthesis

The OSMES workflow screened AlphaFold-modeled vitamin-B6-dependent enzymes by docking the substrate into active sites and ranking catalytically favorable conformations. It identified human SHMT1/2 as enzymes with hydroxytrimethyllysine-aldolase activity, validated in vitro; mouse Tha1 showed much higher catalytic efficiency, supporting a species difference in the pathway. The authors infer that humans may have lost a dedicated gene, with SHMT activity partly compensating.

Technology: OSMES (One Substrate–Many Enzymes Screening), automated structure-based docking/ranking workflow using AlphaFold models

Tree-based machine learning nominates Parabacteroides merdae and N-acetyl-D-mannosamine as ulcerative-colitis alleviators in a mouse model

The researchers combined personalized gut-microbiome community metabolic models from human iHMP data with tree-based machine-learning feature selection to identify microbial strains and metabolites associated with ulcerative-colitis samples. Parabacteroides merdae ATCC 43,184 and N-acetyl-D-mannosamine were among the most predictive features; the strain was also the main modeled microbial contributor to the metabolite. In a DSS-induced mouse colitis experiment, oral administration of the bacterium or metabolite improved reported colitis measures.

Technology: Tree-based supervised classifiers including random forest, gradient-boosted regression trees, XGBoost, XGBoost-random forest and LightGBM, combined with individualized genome-scale microbiome community models

DIP-MS combines deep-learning-based signal processing with native-gel fractionation and mass spectrometry to resolve complex isoforms. 5 Mar 2024Max Planck Institute of Biochemistry

An ML-generated MinE homolog restores bacterial cell division patterning in vivo

A machine-learning-generated MinE homolog, synMinEv25, restored Min oscillations and wild-type-like cell growth and morphology when substituted for the native minE gene in an E. coli strain lacking minDE. Researchers generated variants with a multiple-sequence-alignment variational autoencoder, used AlphaFold2-based structural scoring to select candidates, and screened them in synthetic-cell mimics before in-vivo testing. The validated variant is a distant homolog with less than 50% sequence identity to E. coli MinE.

Technology: Multiple-sequence-alignment variational autoencoder (MSA-VAE), AlphaFold2 Multimer, and a structure-based divide-and-conquer scoring pipeline

  • Tested: Researchers generated variants with a multiple-sequence-alignment variational autoencoder, used AlphaFold2-based structural scoring to select candidates, and screened them in synthetic-cell mimics before in-vivo testing.

AI generated and prioritized candidates, while wet-lab experiments established function.

30 Jan 2024Konkuk University

Machine-learning models identify carbon-source-dependent metabolic reactions that control Escherichia coli growth

Elastic-net and multilayer-perceptron models integrated genome-wide gene-deletion data and simulated fluxes to predict beneficial and detrimental reactions for E. coli growth on 30 carbon sources. Beyond training-set examples, the authors experimentally validated model predictions about the glyoxylate shunt, pyruvate dehydrogenase, and redundant purine-biosynthesis reactions. 5 Dec 2023Weill Cornell Medicine; New York Genome Center; collaborating institutions

3D-HiChAT predicts embryonic enhancer hubs validated by CRISPR interference

A random-forest model integrating ATAC-seq, H3K27ac ChIP-seq and HiChIP data predicted enhancer effects across mouse trophoblast stem cells, embryonic stem cells and extraembryonic endoderm cells. CRISPRi tests supported a shared Enh14 hub regulating both Tfcp2l1 and Gli2 in embryonic stem cells, and an Enh4 hub linked to seven genes in extraembryonic endoderm; four of those seven showed expression reductions as predicted. Across 40 tested enhancer-promoter pairs, experiments yielded 12 true positives and 13 true negatives, with overall prediction accuracy 0.71.

2 Dec 2023ETH Zurich; Institute of Microbiology

Machine learning identifies bacterial strains and combinations that reduce pathogen colonization in Arabidopsis

Random forest and elastic-net models analyzed 136 synthetic communities, each composed of five bacterial strains, and identified strain identity as the strongest predictor of protection against Pseudomonas syringae DC3000 in Arabidopsis. Independent test communities confirmed predictive performance, and follow-up plant assays showed that Acidovorax Leaf76, Rhizobium Leaf68 and Pseudomonas Leaf15 each reduced pathogen colonization by about two orders of magnitude versus axenic controls; a Rhizobium Leaf371–Arthrobacter Leaf337 combination also reduced colonization.

Technology: Random forest and elastic-net generalized linear models (GLMNet)

Researchers tested the leading strains and combinations in additional plant experiments, validating the model-prioritized protection results.

18 Oct 2023University of Chicago Pritzker School of Molecular Engineering; University of Chicago Cellular Screening Center

Machine-learning-guided screening discovers potent innate immune signaling modulators

At the University of Chicago, an active-learning pipeline iteratively selected molecules from a 139,998-compound library for in-vitro assays of NF-κB and interferon regulatory factor (IRF) signaling. After screening about 2% of the library, the study reported compounds suppressing NF-κB up to 15-fold or elevating NF-κB and IRF up to 5-fold and 6-fold, respectively. Follow-up cytokine assays found PME-4007 increased IFN-β secretion about threefold when co-delivered with the STING agonist cGAMP.

Technology: Machine-learning active-learning pipeline with variational-autoencoder molecular representations, Gaussian-process regression and Bayesian optimization

21 Aug 2023University of Texas at Austin; Dell Medical School; Columbia University; New York Genome Center

Deep-learning knee imaging phenotypes reveal osteoarthritis genetic and fracture associations

Using knee DXA scans from the UK Biobank, a ResNet-101 model identified 1,931 more probable knee-osteoarthritis cases than health-record coding and a U-Net segmentation model estimated minimum joint-space width (mJSW). In 29,257 participants, the quantitative mJSW phenotype had substantially higher SNP heritability than the binary phenotypes and yielded 18 genome-wide-significant loci, compared with one for the image-based binary classifier and six for ICD-10 coding; only one mJSW locus was already significant in a large prior knee-OA GWAS. The AI-derived mJSW phenotype was also associated with fracture history in an epidemiological analysis, a signal not seen with the binary case labels.

Technology: ResNet-101 convolutional neural network for knee-OA classification and U-Net image segmentation for joint-space measurement

Relevance 53 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Using knee DXA scans from the UK Biobank, a ResNet-101 model identified 1,931 more probable knee-osteoarthritis cases than health-record coding and a U-Net segmentation model estimated minimum joint-space width (mJSW).
  • Still unknown: The GWAS used 29,257 quality-controlled UK Biobank participants with knee DXA images, all from the analyzed white-British ancestry subset; ancestry transferability is unestablished. DXA images were non-weight-bearing. The 18 loci are genome-wide associations for a model-derived quantitative endophenotype, not 18 proven causal OA genes; the paper notes only one also reached significance in a previous large knee-OA GWAS and many loci overlap broad anthropometric associations. The fracture result is observational and does not show that OA or joint-space narrowing causes fractures. The medRxiv preprint DOI 10.1101/2023.03.07.23286909 is an earlier version of this same study and is not counted separately.
  • AI role: The neural networks converted routine knee DXA scans into two disease phenotypes: a case–control OA classifier and a quantitative minimum joint-space-width measurement. The researchers used these AI-derived phenotypes in GWAS and downstream epidemiological association analyses, increasing statistical power and revealing genetic and fracture associations that were not significant with ICD-10 case coding or the binary classifier.

12 Jun 2023Gladstone Institutes; University of California, San Francisco; Chan Zuckerberg Biohub San Francisco

SIMMER-guided experiments identify gut bacterial strains that deplete methotrexate

SIMMER combines reaction-chemistry and protein-sequence similarity to predict gut microbial species and enzymes responsible for drug biotransformations. For methotrexate (MTX), the model predicted candidate strains and enzyme sequences; researchers tested 42 gut bacterial isolates by HPLC. SIMMER predicted 13 isolates as MTX-metabolizing, and 10 of those tested isolates showed MTX depletion; the association was significant in the study (Fisher exact p=0.046). Predicted MTX-hydrolysis enzyme signatures were also enriched in stool samples of MTX nonresponders in an analyzed rheumatoid-arthritis cohort.

Technology: SIMMER, a computational reaction-similarity and protein-homology pipeline using chemical fingerprints, MetaCyc reactions and gut metagenomic sequences

Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: For methotrexate (MTX), the model predicted candidate strains and enzyme sequences; researchers tested 42 gut bacterial isolates by HPLC.

BacterAI experimentally maps amino-acid growth requirements of two oral Streptococcus species

BacterAI autonomously designed and ran growth experiments with robotic liquid handling to learn amino-acid requirements for the oral commensals Streptococcus gordonii and S. sanguinis.   • Tested: In vitro HPLC–MS and enzyme-kinetic experiments also corrected the annotation of MJ1651 and confirmed the multiple activities of SsFIA.
  • Still unknown: The study validated a small, selected set of proteins, and also showed CLEAN can assign functions to non-enzymes; the demonstrated result is specific to the tested proteins. TTHA0338 is one of multiple results reported by this paper; record describes the previously unknown function rather than treating every model benchmark as a separate discovery.
  • AI role: CLEAN predicted enzyme commission functions from protein sequences; researchers then validated the predicted activity experimentally. It was the model that nominated TTHA0338 as a SAM hydrolase, not an autonomous discovery or validation system.

8 Feb 2023Washington University in St. Louis; collaborating institutions

CellOracle-guided zebrafish perturbations identify axial-mesoderm effects of noto and lhx1a loss

The CellOracle machine-learning workflow used single-cell gene-regulatory networks to prioritize transcription factors for perturbation in zebrafish development. CRISPR/Cas9 tests confirmed a model-predicted, previously unreported increase in prechordal-plate differentiation after loss of noto, alongside the known loss of notochord. Model-prioritized lhx1a loss also inhibited axial-mesoderm differentiation: single-cell RNA-seq found expansion of early notochord cells and reductions in later notochord and prechordal-plate populations; orthogonal RNA assays supported reduced notochord/prechordal markers.

Technology: CellOracle, a machine-learning gene-regulatory-network model with in-silico transcription-factor perturbation

3 Oct 2022University of Toronto; RIKEN Center for Sustainable Resource Science, and collaborators

BIONIC links the natural product NP329 to yeast cell-wall biogenesis and β-1,6-glucan synthesis

The BIONIC graph-convolutional network integrated yeast interaction datasets and predicted essential-gene chemical sensitivities to the uncharacterized compound NP329. Experimental screens supported four BIONIC-predicted sensitive genes in the cell-wall biogenesis process; follow-up tests showed related steroidal alkaloid jervine reduced β-1,6-glucan and KRE6/SKN1 mutations conferred resistance, supporting a pathway-level connection for this compound family.

Technology: BIONIC (Biological Network Integration using Convolutions), a graph-convolutional deep-learning network-integration method

15 Sep 2022Cedars-Sinai Medical Center; Medical College of Wisconsin; Stockholm University; University of Gothenburg

MIMaL predicts and experimentally validates five yeast gene functions from protein–metabolite profiles

MIMaL used tree-based regression and SHAP interpretation on yeast proteome/metabolome measurements, then clustered protein-control profiles across single-gene knockouts. It predicted functions for five genes, with experimental validation reported for all five. Two previously uncharacterized genes, YJR120W and YDL157C, were predicted to modulate mitochondrial translation; functions were also predicted and validated for SDH9, ISC1 and FMP52. The study additionally validated previously unrecognized protein links to citrate.

Technology: MIMaL: ExtraTrees regression of metabolite abundance from proteomic profiles, SHAP interpretation, and UMAP/OPTICS clustering of protein-control profiles

13 Jan 2022Helmholtz Munich; Wellcome Sanger Institute

Cell2location: mapping fine-grained cell types in spatial transcriptomics

Cell2location is a Bayesian model that resolves fine-grained cell types in spatial transcriptomics data by accounting for technical variation and sharing information across locations. Applied to three tissues, it revealed regional astrocyte subtypes in mouse brain, a rare pre-germinal center B cell population in human lymph node, and fine immune populations in human gut lymphoid follicles. Caveat: the reported results are biological findings from the method's application, with no single quantitative accuracy figure confirmed in the abstract.

Technology: Cell2location (Bayesian deep probabilistic model)

27 Oct 2021University of Oxford; Harvard Medical School

EVE: evolutionary model predicts disease-variant pathogenicity without labels

EVE, an unsupervised deep generative model trained on evolutionary sequence variation across about 140,000 species, predicted pathogenicity for more than 36 million variants in 3,219 disease genes and classified over 256,000 variants of unknown significance. The authors report it outperforms methods trained on labelled disease data and performs on par with high-throughput experimental predictions. Caveat: the authors state EVE is not a diagnostic test.

Technology: EVE (deep generative evolutionary model, variational autoencoder)
