4 Oct 2021Google DeepMind

Enformer: gene expression predicted from DNA sequence

A Transformer-based model predicts gene expression from 200,000 base pairs of input DNA, over five times more than earlier methods, and is much more accurate at predicting variant effects. Enhancer attribution scores are comparable to methods using experimental data. The post gives no numerical gain and acknowledges that linking enhancers to the genes they regulate remains an open problem.

24 Sep 2021New York University; Purdue University and University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign

Cross-species machine learning identifies transcription factors that negatively regulate plant nitrogen-use efficiency

Evolutionarily informed XGBoost and GENIE3 analyses of conserved nitrogen-response expression patterns prioritized transcription factors associated with nitrogen-use efficiency in Arabidopsis and maize. Loss-of-function tests found higher NUE in Arabidopsis myb33 and tcp2 mutants under both low- and high-nitrogen conditions, and in six other tested TF mutants specifically under low nitrogen; a maize nfya3 mutant also showed higher nitrogen-use efficiency under sufficient nitrogen in field tests.

19 Aug 2021California Institute of Technology

Machine learning-assisted directed evolution with in silico combinatorial screening

A path-independent machine-learning protocol screens full combinatorial protein libraries in silico, with training-set design that avoids near-zero-fitness variants. On a four-site epistatic fitness landscape the optimized protocol reached the global fitness maximum up to 81-fold more often than single-step greedy optimization. Caveat: validation is on a combinatorial fitness landscape dataset, not a new wet-lab evolution campaign.

Technology: Machine-learning-assisted directed evolution (in silico combinatorial screening)

Machine learning reveals a nitrogen-assimilation strategy in Bacteroides

A microbiome metabolomics study used random-forest models to associate bacterial metabolite profiles with taxonomy and found a previously undescribed nitrogen-assimilation pattern in Bacteroides/Bacteroidetes. Follow-up growth experiments tested 60 Bacteroidetes strains with asparagine or glutamine as the sole nitrogen source and supported the predicted phenotype; the study also reports a distinct reduced-sulfur utilization result in B. thetaiotaomicron.

1 Jul 2021Academia Sinica, Taiwan

iTARGEX analysis identifies ELG1 as a regulator of yeast transcription buffering and four protein-turnover regulators

iTARGEX applied a mixture-regression model to gene-expression profiles from 1,484 single-gene deletion yeast mutants, associating transcriptomic changes with S-phase transcriptional buffering and global protein turnover. Experiments supported ELG1 as a previously unrecognized regulator of transcriptional buffering during S phase; the phenotype was reproduced in a second genetic background and rescued by a PCNA-D150E mutation. Follow-up protein-turnover measurements showed altered turnover in MRN1, UPF3, RAM1 and SSN3 deletion strains.

Technology: iTARGEX, a finite-mixture regression framework fitted by expectation maximization to single-gene-deletion transcriptomic data

8 Jun 2021University of Oxford; University of Coimbra; University of North Carolina at Greensboro; University of Algarve; Gorongosa National Park

Satellite-image clustering guides discovery of four late Miocene fossil sites in Mozambique’s Urema Rift

Unsupervised k-means clustering of seven Landsat 8 spectral bands highlighted a small set of priority areas for paleontological field survey in Gorongosa National Park. The 2018 campaign documented four new late Miocene fossil localities; three lay within the model’s target cluster and the fourth was less than 90 m away in the same gully system. Together, the sites expose the first reported estuarine coastal-forest fossil setting in the East African Rift System, filling a regional paleoenvironmental gap.

Technology: Unsupervised k-means clustering of Landsat 8 imagery

Relevance 60 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: Unsupervised k-means clustering of seven Landsat 8 spectral bands highlighted a small set of priority areas for paleontological field survey in Gorongosa National Park.
  • Still unknown: This was a pilot study in a 36 km² area and the model had notable false positives: reported precision was 55.56%, despite 84.6% overall accuracy. Three of four new localities overlapped the selected cluster; the fourth was less than 90 m away and was found by surveying the same gully system, so model localization for that site is indirect. The findings are four new fossil localities, not four new hominin taxa; the reported estuarine coastal-forest interpretation is a paleoenvironmental inference.
  • AI role: The authors clustered seven spectral bands without pre-labeled land-cover classes, compared clusters with known fossil localities, and selected the cluster containing most known sites as a predictive target for field prospecting. This decision-support workflow directed the 2018 field survey that documented the new localities; a random forest was separately used to estimate spectral-band importance.

12 Apr 2021Ghent University; VIB Center for Plant Systems Biology; Bioinformatics Institute Ghent

An integrated Arabidopsis regulatory network predicts 13 previously unlinked reactive-oxygen-stress regulators

Researchers integrated DNA-motif, chromatin-accessibility, TF-binding and expression evidence with supervised learning to build an Arabidopsis gene-regulatory network of 1,491 transcription factors and 1.7 million TF–target interactions. The network predicted candidate reactive-oxygen-species (ROS) regulators; loss- and gain-of-function phenotyping supported 13 regulators not previously linked to ROS or stress responses. Newly tested examples included WRKY55, WRKY47, AT2G33710 and AT5G57150. The paper also reports that 75% of its ROS-predicted TFs had ROS or physiological stress functions supported by either this study or prior evidence.

Technology: Supervised-learning integrated gene-regulatory network (iGRN) built from motif, open-chromatin, transcription-factor-binding and expression-based networks

19 Jan 2021Janelia Research Campus, Howard Hughes Medical Institute; National Institute of Neurological Disorders and Stroke, NIH

AI-photoswitchable CRISPRi screening identifies new regulators of TFEB localization during starvation

An image-classification-guided pooled CRISPRi screen mapped factors that affect TFEB movement from nucleus to cytosol during prolonged starvation. The screen identified multiple candidate genes, and follow-up assays independently validated 21 of 64 neural-network-called hits; experiments on CREB5 further linked it to TFEB retention and starvation-associated autophagy/lysosomal protein expression.

Technology: AI-photoswitchable screening (AI-PS), using SVM classifiers and a convolutional neural network

  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Language models trained on about 60,000 HIV, 45,000 influenza and 4,000 coronavirus sequences were checked against earlier findings on influenza and HIV; the SARS-CoV-2 analysis is described as not yet peer-reviewed.
  • Still unknown: Flagged SARS-CoV-2 sequences still need investigation, and it is unknown how fast SARS-CoV-2 mutates, so how long current vaccines stay effective is unclear.
  • AI role: Adapted language-model methods to viral protein sequences to predict surface regions more or less likely to enable immune escape, as candidate vaccine targets.

Evidence sources: https://news.mit.edu/2021/model-viruses-escape-immune-0114 · https://www.ebi.ac.uk/europepmc/webservices/rest/search?query=DOI:10.1126/science.abd7331&resultType=core&format=json

30 Nov 2020Google DeepMind

AlphaFold wins CASP14 for protein folding

AlphaFold achieved a median GDT score of 92.4 across the target set (about 1.6 Å mean error) and 87.0 on the hardest targets, which organizers judged a solution to the problem of single-protein structures. DeepMind notes that protein complexes and interactions with other molecules remain open challenges.

Technology: AlphaFold 2

Family AlphaFold: profile · models page

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Announcement only
  • Test type: prospective or independent check
  • Tested: Blind assessment at CASP14 on recently determined protein structures: median GDT of 92.4 across targets and 87.0 on the hardest free-modelling targets, judged by the organizers as a solution to single-protein structure prediction.
  • Still unknown: Protein complexes, interactions with DNA, RNA or small molecules, and full side-chain placement remain open; not every predicted structure will be perfect.
  • AI role: Attention-based neural network using evolutionary sequence alignments and residue-pair representations to predict 3D protein structure from sequence.

Evidence sources: https://deepmind.google/blog/alphafold-a-solution-to-a-50-year-old-grand-challenge-in-biology/

12 Oct 2020UC San Francisco; Gladstone Institutes

Akita: predicting 3D genome folding from DNA sequence

Akita, a convolutional network, predicts chromatin contact maps for about 1 Mb of DNA sequence. On held-out data it reaches a Pearson R of 0.61 and Spearman R of 0.56, and it learns an orientation-specific grammar for CTCF binding sites. Caveat: the predictions are computational; the paper applies them to in silico mutagenesis, eQTL interpretation and structural-variant prediction.

Technology: Akita (convolutional neural network, Basenji-based trunk)

  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Akita's contact-map predictions were evaluated on held-out data and applied in silico to mutagenesis, eQTL interpretation and structural-variant prediction.
  • Still unknown: Not reported in the abstract read; the predictions are computational and no experimental validation is described there.
  • AI role: A convolutional network predicting chromatin contact maps from about 1 Mb of DNA sequence, which learned an orientation-specific CTCF binding grammar.

21 May 2020University of California, Berkeley; Monash University; University of Melbourne

AcRanker-guided discovery of AcrIIA20 and AcrIIA21 anti-CRISPR proteins

AcRanker, an XGBoost ranking model using amino-acid sequence features, ranked proteins from prophage regions in self-targeting Streptococcus genomes. Experimental testing identified two previously unknown type II-A anti-CRISPRs: AcrIIA20 (ML1), which strongly inhibited Streptococcus iniae Cas9 and weakly inhibited S. pyogenes Cas9, and AcrIIA21 (ML8), which inhibited S. pyogenes, S. aureus and S. iniae Cas9 with lower potency.

Technology: AcRanker, an XGBoost pairwise ranking model trained on amino-acid composition and grouped di- and tri-mer features

Relevance 60 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: Experimental testing identified two previously unknown type II-A anti-CRISPRs: AcrIIA20 (ML1), which strongly inhibited Streptococcus iniae Cas9 and weakly inhibited S. pyogenes Cas9, and AcrIIA21 (ML8), which inhibited S. pyogenes, S. aureus and S. iniae Cas9 with lower potency.
  • Still unknown: The authors selected 10 candidates for follow-up, successfully purified nine and reported three validated candidates across their experiments, including ML3/AcrIIA12, which had also been identified separately as an LmoCas9 inhibitor during the study. AcrIIA20 and AcrIIA21 were roughly tenfold less potent against SpyCas9 than the established AcrIIA4 control; tested potency and host range are assay-specific. The 2025 Evo paper reports distinct generated proteins EvoAcr1–5 and separate assays; shared anti-CRISPR function does not make these identical discoveries.
  • AI role: The model ranked candidate proteins from predicted prophage regions for anti-CRISPR likelihood; the researchers selected candidates from those rankings and biochemically tested them. It directly prioritized the proteins later named AcrIIA20 and AcrIIA21, whose Cas9 inhibition was measured in vitro.

Evidence sources: https://academic.oup.com/nar/article/48/9/4698/5819938

6 Mar 2020University of Oxford; University of Zurich

KCML links olfactory-receptor perturbations to multicellular organization and colorectal-cancer signaling

Knowledge- and Context-driven Machine Learning (KCML) analyzed perturbation phenotypes across molecular, cellular and population scales. In colorectal-cancer cells, it associated depletion of olfactory receptors and TGFβ/WNT pathway genes with abnormal multicellular organization. The authors integrated the predictions with patient data and found olfactory-receptor expression correlated with WNT/TGFβ signatures and worse outcomes in higher-grade tumors.

Technology: KCML, weakly supervised machine-learning framework using support-vector-machine classifiers trained on gene-ontology annotations and high-throughput perturbation phenotypes

Relevance 53 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: retrospective or benchmark
  • Tested: Knowledge- and Context-driven Machine Learning (KCML) analyzed perturbation phenotypes across molecular, cellular and population scales.
  • Still unknown: Patient data validate expression associations and outcome stratification, not a causal olfactory-receptor mechanism in tumors. The authors describe KCML as hypothesis-generating; predictions can be indirect through interacting genes. This is a distinct paper/result from later AI cancer models.
  • AI role: Mapped gene-perturbation morphology to context-specific gene functions and nominated olfactory-receptor links to multicellular organization; patient-expression analyses provided an independent association check.

4 Mar 2020Google DeepMind

Predicted structures of SARS-CoV-2 proteins

DeepMind openly shared AlphaFold predictions, without waiting for review, for six poorly studied viral proteins (membrane, ORF3a, Nsp2, Nsp4, Nsp6, and the C-terminal domain of papain-like protease). They were not experimentally verified; an ORF3a experimental structure deposited in the PDB in June 2020 matched the second predicted model well.

Technology: AlphaFold

  • Tested: Predictions compared with experimental structures: the spike prediction matched the PDB structure, and the second-ranked ORF3a model agreed well with later experimental work.
  • Still unknown: Predictions were not experimentally verified overall; the system was still in development and accuracy is not certain; released before peer review; the top ORF3a model misplaced some transmembrane helices.

22 Jan 2020University of California San Diego; and collaborators

Interpretable multi-omics machine learning reveals unexpected drug mechanisms in Huntington disease models

An interpretable machine-learning model integrated transcriptomic, epigenomic, metabolomic and proteomic data from Huntington-disease cell models to identify previously unknown mechanisms for protective compounds. The analysis predicted autophagy activation for one compound group and mitochondrial-respiration inhibition for another; follow-up imaging, biochemical and energetics assays experimentally confirmed these effects in the cell model.

Technology: Interpretable network-optimization model integrating multi-omics over a molecular-interaction network

Relevance 60 biology Original sourcePaper Suggest a correction
Source checked · editorial details

The source page was checked against this entry. Independent confirmation is not assessed by this label.

Source type: paper. Paper: linked separately.

Evidence context (descriptions follow the cited sources; test type is a catalog classification):

  • Publication status: Peer-reviewed paper
  • Test type: direct test
  • Tested: The analysis predicted autophagy activation for one compound group and mitochondrial-respiration inhibition for another; follow-up imaging, biochemical and energetics assays experimentally confirmed these effects in the cell model.
  • Still unknown: Findings were experimentally established in STHdhQ111 murine striatal cells, with autophagy effects also checked in human SH-SY5Y and HEK293 cell lines. The paper notes that the model prioritizes pathways but does not resolve every causal step or demonstrate therapeutic efficacy in patients.
  • AI role: The model integrated multiple omics and prioritized cellular pathways as candidate mechanisms of action; researchers tested those predictions with cellular imaging, biochemical assays and energetics measurements. It revealed unexpected mechanisms for compounds with unrelated known targets.

Evidence sources: https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6976599/
